11/27/25, 11:57 AM - Abhay raj Jain Csd Clg: Title Slide: Project title, your name and roll number, guide's name, department, and year.

Table of Contents: A list of all the topics you will cover in the presentation.

Introduction: Briefly introduce the project's topic, the problem it addresses, and why it's significant.

Aim and Objectives: Clearly state the project's goals and what you aim to achieve

Methodology/Approach: Explain the methods, processes, and techniques you used to complete the project. Include diagrams, flowcharts, or block diagrams to illustrate your approach.

Tools and Technologies: List the hardware and software tools you used.

Implementation: Detail the actual process of building or creating the project, with sample outputs or screenshots if possible.

Results and Discussion: Present your findings, interpret them, and discuss how they relate back to your objectives.

Applications: Explain the current and potential uses of your project.

Conclusion: Summarize the key achievements of your project.

Future Scope: Suggest potential improvements or further work that could be done.

References: List all sources you cited in a consistent format.

Thank You: A final slide to thank the audience.

12/11/25, 9:44 PM - Abhay raj Jain Csd Clg: The primary components of classic "straight dynamite" are:

Nitroglycerin (Glycerol trinitrate): The main explosive component, an oily liquid that is highly sensitive to shock.

Absorbent Material (Dope): Historically, this was kieselguhr (diatomaceous earth), a porous, fossilized material that "soaks up" the nitroglycerin. Modern alternatives, which can contribute to the explosive power, include wood pulp, sawdust, or starch.

Antacid: A small amount of a substance like calcium carbonate is added to neutralize any acidity in the absorbent material, which helps prevent the mixture from becoming unstable over time.

Paraffin enclosure: The final mixture is wrapped in paraffin paper to protect it from moisture.

3/25/26, 7:56 PM - ♕♡⚘𝐴ₘᵅᶰ𝑟ᤂ𝔧ῥข𝐭⚘♡♕: #include <graphics.h>
#include <conio.h>
#include <math.h> // Required for sin/cos

void drawSun() {
    setcolor(YELLOW);
    setfillstyle(SOLID_FILL, YELLOW);
    circle(580, 60, 40);
    floodfill(580, 60, YELLOW);
    // Draw rays properly using basic trig
    for(int i = 0; i < 360; i += 30) {
        double rad = i * 3.14 / 180;
        line(580, 60, 580 + 60 * cos(rad), 60 + 60 * sin(rad));
    }
}

void drawCloud(int x, int y) {
    // 1. Set colors
    setcolor(WHITE);
    setfillstyle(SOLID_FILL, WHITE);

    // 2. Draw the three overlapping circles
    circle(x, y, 20);           // Left part
    circle(x + 20, y - 5, 25);   // Middle/Top part
    circle(x + 40, y, 20);       // Right part

    // 3. Fill each one specifically
    // We "click" inside the center of each circle to bypass the internal walls
    floodfill(x-5, y+5, WHITE);
    floodfill(x, y, WHITE);               // Fills the left circle
    floodfill(x + 20, y - 5, WHITE); 
    floodfill(x+25, y-5, WHITE);     // Fills the middle circle
    floodfill(x + 45, y, WHITE);  
    floodfill(x+20, y+5, WHITE);        // Fills the right circle
}

void drawStickMan(int x, int y, int step) {
    setcolor(WHITE);
    circle(x, y, 10); // Head
    line(x, y + 10, x, y + 40); // Body
    line(x, y + 20, x - 10, y + 30); // Arms
    line(x, y + 20, x + 10, y + 30);
    
    if (step % 2 == 0) {
        line(x, y + 40, x - 10, y + 60); 
        line(x, y + 40, x + 10, y + 60); 
    } else {
        line(x, y + 40, x - 5, y + 60); 
        line(x, y + 40, x + 5, y + 60);      
    }
}

void drawLady(int x, int y) {
    setcolor(WHITE);
    circle(x, y, 10);
    line(x, y + 10, x - 15, y + 45); // Dress
    line(x - 15, y + 45, x + 15, y + 45);
    line(x + 15, y + 45, x, y + 10);
    line(x, y + 20, x - 15, y + 25); // Arms
    line(x, y + 20, x + 15, y + 25);
    line(x - 5, y + 45, x - 5, y + 60); // Legs
    line(x + 5, y + 45, x + 5, y + 60);
}

int main() {
    int gd = DETECT, gm;
    initgraph(&gd, &gm, (char*)"");

    int manX = 50;
    int ladyX = 400;
    int groundY = 200;

    // 1. WALKING ANIMATION
    for (int i = 0; manX < ladyX - 70; i++) {
        cleardevice();
        drawSun(); 
        drawCloud(100, 80); 
        drawCloud(300, 50);
        line(0, groundY + 60, 640, groundY + 60);
        
        drawStickMan(manX, groundY, i); 
        drawLady(ladyX, groundY);
        
        manX += 10; // This makes him move!
        delay(100); 
    }

    // --- DIALOGUE SEQUENCE ---

    // 1. Man says Hello
    outtextxy(manX, groundY - 30, (char*)"Hello!");
    delay(1500);

    outtextxy(ladyX - 10, groundY - 30, (char*)"Hi");
    delay(1500);

    outtextxy(manX - 50, groundY - 30, (char*)"i like you        ");
    delay(1500);

    // 2. Lady asks: "What do you do?"
    cleardevice();
    drawSun(); drawCloud(100, 80); drawCloud(300, 50);
    line(0, groundY + 60, 640, groundY + 60);
    drawStickMan(manX, groundY, 0); drawLady(ladyX, groundY);
    outtextxy(ladyX - 40, groundY - 30, (char*)"What do you do?");
    delay(1500);

    // 3. Man replies: "I am an engineer"
    outtextxy(manX - 50, groundY - 50, (char*)"I am an Engineer");
    delay(1500);

    // 4. The "Sorry"
    cleardevice();
    drawSun(); drawCloud(100, 80); drawCloud(300, 50);
    line(0, groundY + 60, 640, groundY + 60);
    drawStickMan(manX, groundY, 0); drawLady(ladyX, groundY);
    setcolor(RED);
    outtextxy(ladyX, groundY - 30, (char*)"Sorry.");
    delay(1000);

    // --- LIGHTNING EFFECT ---
    for(int j = 0; j < 3; j++) {
        setbkcolor(WHITE); cleardevice(); // Flash
        delay(50);
        setbkcolor(BLACK); cleardevice(); // Dark
        setcolor(YELLOW);
        line(300, 0, 320, 50); line(320, 50, 290, 100); line(290, 100, 310, 180); // Lightning bolt
        
        setcolor(WHITE);
        line(0, groundY + 60, 640, groundY + 60);
        drawStickMan(manX, groundY, 0);
        drawLady(ladyX, groundY);
        delay(150);
    }
    outtextxy(250, 50, (char*)"* THUNDER RUMBLES *");

    getch();
    closegraph();
    return 0;
}

4/19/26, 11:00 AM - ♕♡⚘𝐴ₘᵅᶰ𝑟ᤂ𝔧ῥข𝐭⚘♡♕: #include <iostream>
#include <vector>
#include <algorithm>
#include <ctime>
#include <cstdlib>

using namespace std;

// Structure to represent a Polygon (Triangle)
struct Polygon {
    int id;
    float z_depth; // Simplified: average depth for sorting
    int color;
};

// Comparator for Painter's Algorithm (Sorting Back-to-Front)
bool compareDepth(Polygon a, Polygon b) {
    return a.z_depth > b.z_depth;
}

void evaluatePerformance(int numPolygons) {
    vector<Polygon> scene;
    
    // Generate a random scene
    for (int i = 0; i < numPolygons; i++) {
        Polygon p;
        p.id = i;
        p.z_depth = static_cast<float>(rand() % 1000);
        p.color = rand() % 15;
        scene.push_back(p);
    }

    cout << "--- Evaluating Scene with " << numPolygons << " Polygons ---" << endl;

    // 1. PAINTER'S ALGORITHM EVALUATION
    // Primary overhead: Sorting O(N log N)
    clock_t startPainter = clock();
    sort(scene.begin(), scene.end(), compareDepth);
    // Simulation of "drawing" each polygon
    for(int i = 0; i < numPolygons; i++) {
        // In real graphics, this involves filling pixels
    }
    clock_t endPainter = clock();
    double timePainter = double(endPainter - startPainter) / CLOCKS_PER_SEC;

    // 2. Z-BUFFER ALGORITHM EVALUATION
    // Primary overhead: Per-pixel/Per-polygon depth check O(N)
    // In hardware, this is highly parallelized
    clock_t startZ = clock();
    for (int i = 0; i < numPolygons; i++) {
        // Simulation of depth testing: 
        // if (pixel_z < z_buffer[x][y]) { update }
        float current_z = scene[i].z_depth; 
    }
    clock_t endZ = clock();
    double timeZ = double(endZ - startZ) / CLOCKS_PER_SEC;

    cout << "Painter's Algorithm (Sorting): " << timePainter << " seconds" << endl;
    cout << "Z-Buffer (Depth Testing):      " << timeZ << " seconds" << endl;
    cout << "------------------------------------------" << endl << endl;
}

int main() {
    srand(time(0));

    // Test with increasing complexity
    evaluatePerformance(1000);
    evaluatePerformance(10000);
    evaluatePerformance(100000);

    return 0;
}

4/30/26, 12:19 PM - ♕♡⚘𝐴ₘᵅᶰ𝑟ᤂ𝔧ῥข𝐭⚘♡♕: #include <graphics.h>
#include <conio.h>
#include <math.h> // Required for sin/cos

void drawSun() {
    setcolor(YELLOW);
    setfillstyle(SOLID_FILL, YELLOW);
    circle(580, 60, 40);
    floodfill(580, 60, YELLOW);
    // Draw rays properly using basic trig
    for(int i = 0; i < 360; i += 30) {
        double rad = i * 3.14 / 180;
        line(580, 60, 580 + 60 * cos(rad), 60 + 60 * sin(rad));
    }
}

void drawCloud(int x, int y) {
    // 1. Set colors
    setcolor(WHITE);
    setfillstyle(SOLID_FILL, WHITE);

    // 2. Draw the three overlapping circles
    circle(x, y, 20);           // Left part
    circle(x + 20, y - 5, 25);   // Middle/Top part
    circle(x + 40, y, 20);       // Right part

    // 3. Fill each one specifically
    // We "click" inside the center of each circle to bypass the internal walls
    floodfill(x-5, y+5, WHITE);
    floodfill(x, y, WHITE);               // Fills the left circle
    floodfill(x + 20, y - 5, WHITE); 
    floodfill(x+25, y-5, WHITE);     // Fills the middle circle
    floodfill(x + 45, y, WHITE);  
    floodfill(x+20, y+5, WHITE);        // Fills the right circle
}

void drawStickMan(int x, int y, int step) {
    setcolor(WHITE);
    circle(x, y, 10); // Head
    line(x, y + 10, x, y + 40); // Body
    line(x, y + 20, x - 10, y + 30); // Arms
    line(x, y + 20, x + 10, y + 30);
    
    if (step % 2 == 0) {
        line(x, y + 40, x - 10, y + 60); 
        line(x, y + 40, x + 10, y + 60); 
    } else {
        line(x, y + 40, x - 5, y + 60); 
        line(x, y + 40, x + 5, y + 60);      
    }
}

void drawLady(int x, int y) {
    setcolor(WHITE);
    circle(x, y, 10);
    line(x, y + 10, x - 15, y + 45); // Dress
    line(x - 15, y + 45, x + 15, y + 45);
    line(x + 15, y + 45, x, y + 10);
    line(x, y + 20, x - 15, y + 25); // Arms
    line(x, y + 20, x + 15, y + 25);
    line(x - 5, y + 45, x - 5, y + 60); // Legs
    line(x + 5, y + 45, x + 5, y + 60);
}

int main() {
    int gd = DETECT, gm;
    initgraph(&gd, &gm, (char*)"");

    int manX = 50;
    int ladyX = 400;
    int groundY = 200;

    // 1. WALKING ANIMATION
    for (int i = 0; manX < ladyX - 70; i++) {
        cleardevice();
        drawSun(); 
        drawCloud(100, 80); 
        drawCloud(300, 50);
        line(0, groundY + 60, 640, groundY + 60);
        
        drawStickMan(manX, groundY, i); 
        drawLady(ladyX, groundY);
        
        manX += 10; // This makes him move!
        delay(100); 
    }

    // --- DIALOGUE SEQUENCE ---

    // 1. Man says Hello
    outtextxy(manX, groundY - 30, (char*)"Hello!");
    delay(1500);

    outtextxy(ladyX - 10, groundY - 30, (char*)"Hi");
    delay(1500);

    outtextxy(manX - 50, groundY - 30, (char*)"i like you        ");
    delay(1500);

    // 2. Lady asks: "What do you do?"
    cleardevice();
    drawSun(); drawCloud(100, 80); drawCloud(300, 50);
    line(0, groundY + 60, 640, groundY + 60);
    drawStickMan(manX, groundY, 0); drawLady(ladyX, groundY);
    outtextxy(ladyX - 40, groundY - 30, (char*)"What do you do?");
    delay(1500);

    // 3. Man replies: "I am an engineer"
    outtextxy(manX - 50, groundY - 50, (char*)"I am an Engineer");
    delay(1500);

    // 4. The "Sorry"
    cleardevice();
    drawSun(); drawCloud(100, 80); drawCloud(300, 50);
    line(0, groundY + 60, 640, groundY + 60);
    drawStickMan(manX, groundY, 0); drawLady(ladyX, groundY);
    setcolor(RED);
    outtextxy(ladyX, groundY - 30, (char*)"Sorry.");
    delay(1000);

    // --- LIGHTNING EFFECT ---
    for(int j = 0; j < 3; j++) {
        setbkcolor(WHITE); cleardevice(); // Flash
        delay(50);
        setbkcolor(BLACK); cleardevice(); // Dark
        setcolor(YELLOW);
        line(300, 0, 320, 50); line(320, 50, 290, 100); line(290, 100, 310, 180); // Lightning bolt
        
        setcolor(WHITE);
        line(0, groundY + 60, 640, groundY + 60);
        drawStickMan(manX, groundY, 0);
        drawLady(ladyX, groundY);
        delay(150);
    }
    outtextxy(250, 50, (char*)"* THUNDER RUMBLES *");

    getch();
    closegraph();
    return 0;
}

8/6/26, 7:45 PM - Abhay raj Jain Csd Clg: The core idea
You're building an AI system that learns to text/reply like you — a "text clone." It reads incoming messages and drafts (or auto-sends, depending on a toggle) replies in your writing style, based on real examples of how you actually text.
How it works (pipeline)
Data: Export your real chat history (WhatsApp, Telegram, Gmail — whatever you use most). This becomes training data: pairs of (message someone sent you) → (how you replied).
Model: Take a pretrained language model (a pre-built AI model already trained on general text, downloaded from Hugging Face) — not built from scratch. Customize it to sound like you specifically.
Two ways to customize:
Prompting (building this first): feed the model a few examples of your style directly in each request, no training involved. Fast to build and test.
Fine-tuning (building this after): actually retrain part of the model on your real chat data so it "bakes in" your style permanently. More powerful, but needs real data and more setup — specifically using a technique called LoRA (lighter, cheaper fine-tuning method).
Platform integration: Connect it to a real messaging platform — likely Telegram, Discord, or Gmail (not WhatsApp/Instagram, since those don't allow this kind of automation officially).
Manual/Auto switch: A toggle — either the AI just drafts a reply for you to approve, or it sends automatically without you checking.
Logging: Keep records of incoming messages, generated replies, and also response-time patterns (how fast you'd normally reply), since that's part of "how you behave," not just what you say.

8/21/26, 1:01 PM - ♕♡⚘𝐴ₘᵅᶰ𝑟ᤂ𝔧ῥข𝐭⚘♡♕: Abhi mujhe mile nhi h
8/25/26, 4:15 PM - Abhay raj Jain Csd Clg: Project Overview: AI-Powered Personalized Auto-Reply System
The Core Problem
The goal is an AI that reads incoming messages and replies the way the user actually would — not like a generic chatbot, but matching their real texting habits (short, casual, mixed-language, different tone per person). Eventually this runs fully offline on-device, with no data leaving the phone/laptop.
Why This Is Hard (and interesting)
Replying "correctly" needs two very different things:
Style — sounding like the user (tone, phrasing, language mixing, message length)
Facts — actually knowing things (is there a test tomorrow? what did this contact last say?)
An AI model, by itself, only ever learns #1 from examples. It has zero access to #2 unless you explicitly feed it real, current data. This project builds both halves and connects them.
Part 1: Data Collection & Style Learning ("Behavior")
What it is: Turning raw chat history into a form an AI can learn "how this person replies" from.
Steps:
Export a chat as a .txt file (WhatsApp, no media)
Parse it — split into date, time, sender, message for every line
Handle real messiness: multi-line messages (glue continuation lines to the previous message), invisible Unicode characters WhatsApp inserts between time and am/pm, deleted/edited system messages, empty messages, oversized copy-pasted content
Group consecutive messages from the same sender into one "block"
Pair blocks: [other person's block] → [user's block] = one training example
Example:
Incoming: "Notes bhej de"
Reply: "Kis unit k"
Save all pairs to a CSV — this becomes the dataset
What it's used for right now: Few-shot prompting — showing an AI model 10-15 real examples, then asking it to generate a reply to a new message in the same style. This has already been tested and works (tested with Gemini API, free tier).
Example result: Given the message "Kal test hai kya?" (is there a test tomorrow?), after seeing 15 real examples, the model replied "Pta nhi" ("don't know") — genuinely matching the user's real short, casual style, even though it doesn't know the actual answer.
Known limitation this reveals: The model is only mimicking style, not answering with real facts. That's exactly what Part 2 solves.
Part 2: Retrieval & Real Knowledge ("RAG")
What it is: Connecting the AI to actual data sources so it can pull real facts before replying, not just guess based on tone.
The core technique — RAG (Retrieval-Augmented Generation):
Code[style examples] + [relevant real facts, retrieved live] + [incoming message] → AI → reply
Sources to connect (read-only access):
Calendar (schedule, tests, deadlines)
Google Drive (files, documents)
Phone/laptop files
Steps:
Connect to one source via its official API (start with Calendar — cleanest, well-documented)
Pull data, convert into a structured/searchable format (e.g., a table: date, event, type)
When a message comes in, decide: does this need retrieval, or just style? (ask the AI itself: "do you have enough info to answer well?")
If yes, retrieve relevant facts and inject them into the prompt alongside style examples
Generate the reply — now both in-style AND factually accurate
Example:
Message: "Kal test hai kya?"
Retrieved fact (from calendar): "OOPS test — tomorrow, 10 AM"
Reply: "Hn" (matches real fact + real style)
Part 3: Per-Contact Personas
What it is: Different people get different reply styles from the same user — texting a professor ≠ texting a close friend. The system should learn a separate "persona" per contact instead of one generic style.
How it works:
First message from a new, unknown contact → fall back to a generic "stranger" persona
As real history builds with that contact, extract chunks of their conversation incrementally (small chunk first; only pull more if that chunk isn't enough context)
Build/refine that contact's specific tone profile (strict/casual/short/long) over time
Cache each contact's profile so the system doesn't need to re-extract everything on every single message
Ask the user to confirm/correct replies occasionally — this feedback keeps refining the persona
Part 4: System Architecture (how it all connects)
Incoming message arrives (via Telegram/Discord/Gmail official API)
System checks: known contact, or stranger?
Retrieves relevant style examples + persona + (if needed) real facts via RAG
AI generates a reply, plus a confidence score
Low confidence → goes to draft mode (needs user approval before sending)
High confidence → can auto-send (if user enabled that mode)
Everything gets logged — the message, the reply, whether the user approved/edited/rejected it — this feeds back into improving the system over time
Part 5: The Long-Term Plan
Right now, testing uses a hosted AI (Gemini API) for speed. The final goal is different: fine-tune a small offline model (Microsoft Phi-3.5-mini-instruct) directly on the user's chat data, so it runs completely locally — no internet, no external API calls, full privacy.

9/3/26, 11:35 AM - Abhay raj Jain Csd Clg: Project Progress Report: EchoText Local AI Assistant

​Author: Abhay Raj Jain
Date: September 3, 2026
Project: EchoText – Offline-First Personal AI Assistant

​1. Project Overview

​EchoText is a privacy-first, offline AI assistant designed to draft replies to incoming messages in the user's authentic texting style. The system architecture separates context retrieval (routing and fetching from local knowledge graphs/calendars) from stylistic generation, gated by a human-in-the-loop safety and confidence scoring system. 

​2. Completed Milestones

​Phase 1: Architecture Validation & Local Environment Setup

​System Initialization: Successfully set up the local Python virtual environment and installed core lightweight dependencies (Flask, PyYAML, networkx). 

​Pipeline Testing: Executed the zero-dependency offline test harness (src/cli_demo.py). Validated the end-to-end flow of the Orchestrator, confirming that intent classification, mock context retrieval, and the Flask review dashboard function correctly. 

​Phase 2: Data Extraction & Pipeline Engineering

​Raw Data Acquisition: Exported real-world conversation data from WhatsApp, yielding an initial dataset of 720 raw messages.

​Custom Parser Development: Engineered a custom Python regex parser to process the raw .txt export. The parser successfully normalized 12-hour AM/PM timestamps, handled narrow no-break Unicode spaces (\u202f), and mapped the unstructured text into the required JSON schema. 

​Data Cleaning & Heuristic Filtering: Executed the data preparation pipeline (src/style/dataset_prep.py). The system applied the _looks_like_new_topic heuristic to filter out "initiator mismatches" (e.g., replies sent hours later that start new topics rather than continuing the conversation). 

​Final Dataset Generation: Successfully distilled the 720 raw messages into 183 high-quality, back-and-forth training pairs formatted into data/train_pairs.jsonl. 

​3. Current Status: Phase 3 (Machine Learning)

​The project is actively in the machine learning phase.

​Dependency Installation: Successfully installed the necessary deep learning environments (torch, transformers, peft, bitsandbytes, accelerate, datasets). 

​Active Training: Currently executing the src/style/lora_finetune.py script. The base model (microsoft/Phi-3.5-mini-instruct) is being fine-tuned locally using Low-Rank Adaptation (LoRA) and 8-bit quantization on the 183 custom chat pairs. 

​4. Next Steps & Immediate Action Items

​Adapter Integration: Upon training completion, load the resulting LoRA adapter weights (from data/lora_adapter) into the Orchestrator. This will replace the rule-based fallback generator with the fine-tuned neural network. 

​Model Evaluation: Run the interactive CLI demo to test the fine-tuned model's ability to accurately mimic personal texting style and context blending.

​Live API Wiring: Configure OAuth credentials to replace mock connectors with live Google Calendar integrations and the live Telegram bot adapter.
